1. INTRODUCTION 
1 
Q1: Please state your name, occupation, and business address for the record. 
2 
A1: My name is Bente Villadsen and I am a Principal of The Brattle Group, whose business 
3 
address is One Beacon Street, Suite 2600, Boston, Massachusetts, 02108. 
4 
Q2: Briefly describe your education and professional qualifications. 
5 
A2: I have more than 20 years of experience working with regulated utilities on cost of capital 
6 
and related matters. My practice focuses on cost of capital, regulatory finance, and 
7 
accounting issues. I am the co-author of the text, “Risk and Return for Regulated Industries” 
8 
and a frequent speaker on regulated finance at conferences and webinars. I have testified or 
9 
filed expert reports on cost of capital in Alaska, Arizona, California, Hawaii, Illinois, Iowa, 
10 
Michigan, New Mexico, New York, Oregon, Ohio, and Washington as well as before the 
11 
Bonneville Power Administration, Federal Energy Regulatory Commission (“FERC”), the 
12 
Surface Transportation Board, the Alberta Utilities Commission, the Ontario Energy Board, 
13 
Quebec’s Regie, and Mexico’s Comisión Reguladora de Energía. I have provided white 
14 
papers on cost of capital to the regulators in Australia, Canada, and Europe. I have testified 
15 
or filed testimony on regulatory accounting issues before FERC, the Regulatory Commission 
16 
of Alaska, the Michigan Public Service Commission, the Texas Public Utility Commission 
17

as well as in international and U.S. arbitrations and regularly provide advice to utilities on 
1 
regulatory matters as well as risk management. 
2

3

7 
Q3: What is the purpose of your testimony in this proceeding? 
8 
A3: Consolidated Edison Company of New York (“CECONY” or the “Company”) has asked me 
9 
to estimate the cost of equity that the Commission should allow CECONY an opportunity to 
10 
earn on the equity financed portion of its regulated electric and gas utility rate base in New 
11 
York. Specifically, I performed cost of equity analysis and provide return on equity (“ROE”) 
12 
estimates derived from market data for a proxy group of regulated electric, natural gas, and 
13 
water distribution utilities, and provide additional estimates based on an analysis of allowed 
14 
utility risk premiums. I also consider the relative risk of CECONY and its proposed 
15 
regulatory capital structure ratio to arrive at my recommendation for the allowed Return on 
16 
Equity (“ROE”).  
17 
2. SUMMARY OF CONCLUSIONS 
18 
Q4: Do you have any preliminary comments regarding the appropriate ROE? 
19 
A4: The current determination of CECONY’s allowed ROE takes place during uncertain 
20 
economic and financial conditions due to the ongoing impacts of the COVID-19 pandemic 
21 
(including the discovery of new variants), which has led to extremely low U.S. Treasury 
22 
bond yields, substantial fiscal and monetary initiatives, and 30-year high inflation. 
23 
In the light of the current economic conditions and my results, CECONY’s request for a ROE 
24 
of 10 percent and a capital structure with 50 percent equity is conservative.  My analysis 
25 


--- Page 5 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK  
VILLADSEN DIRECT 
 
 
Page 3 of 57 
results in a recommended range of 10-10.5 percent for ROE after taking into account 
1 
CECONY’s specific risk. 
2 
Preliminarily, I note that the determination of CECONY’s allowed ROE takes place during 
3 
times of great uncertainty for electric and gas utilities.  The COVID-19 pandemic has 
4 
substantially impacted all aspects of society, including customers’ use of electricity and gas, 
5 
and as noted, the period has been characterized by very active fiscal and monetary policy.  
6 
The currently very low interest rate is expected to increase.  Notably, the Federal Reserve 
7 
following its December 14-15, 2021 meeting indicated a slow-down in its support for 
8 
treasury and mortgage-backed securities and stated: 
9 
 In light of inflation developments and the further improvement in the labor 
10 
market, the Committee decided to reduce the monthly pace of its net asset 
11 
purchases by $20 billion for Treasury securities and $10 billion for agency 
12 
mortgage-backed securities. Beginning in January, the Committee will 
13 
increase its holdings of Treasury securities by at least $40 billion per month 
14 
and of agency mortgage-backed securities by at least $20 billion per month. 
15 
The Committee judges that similar reductions in the pace of net asset 
16 
purchases will likely be appropriate each month, but it is prepared to adjust 
17 
the pace of purchases if warranted by changes in the economic outlook.1 
18 
For a utility such as CECONY this means that the Federal Reserve is expecting to undertake 
19 
fewer market transactions to keep treasury / mortgage-backed security prices high (i.e., 
20 
interest rates low).  Therefore, the result is expected to be an increase in interest rates.  Of 
21 
note, this is also the first time the Federal Reserve has acknowledged that inflation is an issue 
22 
for the U.S. economy.2 
23 
As of October 2021, electric and gas utility betas average 0.91 and 0.88, which is non-
24 
trivially higher than the beta estimates as of, for example, November 2018 where electric and 
25 
gas utility betas averaged approximately 0.60 and 0.70, respectively.  Thus, the systemic risk 
26 
of the industry has increased, as has the cost of equity (all else equal). 
27

When evaluating the cost of equity, it is also important to consider business risks to long-
1 
term development for electric and natural gas distribution utilities such as CECONY. 
2 
CECONY is smaller than the average sample company and is facing significant energy and 
3 
climate policy changes in New York State.  The presence of such uncertainty for the industry 
4 
increases business risk and hence the cost of equity.  I further discuss how these and other 
5 
business risk factors affect the cost of equity in Section 6. 
6 
Q5: Please summarize your recommendations for CECONY’s ROE. 
7 
A5: I recommend that CECONY be allowed to earn a return on equity of 10-10.5% percent on 
8 
its regulated rate base at the requested 50% percent equity capital structure. The 10 percent 
9 
ROE requested by CECONY is conservative.   I find that the reasonable range for electric 
10 
utilities is 9.5 to 10.5 percent.  I recommend CECONY’s range be in the upper half of that 
11 
range, 10.0 to 10.5 percent, due to its specific risks.  The return on equity that is being 
12 
determined now is expected to be applicable in future years (e.g., 2023 and beyond), so 
13 
CECONY will be exposed to economic changes over the period for which rates are set.  
14 
Because the allowed return on equity is a nominal return, it includes today’s inflation, but 
15 
going forward the inflation could readily change.  Historically, inflation has impacted not 
16 
only product prices but also the cost of capital.  Therefore, it is important to ensure the ROE 
17 
set in this case considers the likely future cost of equity.3 
18 
The data shows a similar range for gas distribution utilities.  The results from these two 
19 
industries reflects CECONY’s business risk.  This recommendation is based on my 
20 
implementation of standard cost of capital estimation models including two versions each of 
21 
the Discounted Cash Flow (“DCF”) model and the Capital Asset Pricing Model (“CAPM”), 
22 
as well as an Implied Risk Premium analysis and an analysis of CECONY’s business risk.  
23 
Figure 1 below summarizes the model results using the requested 50 percent equity capital 
24 
structure. The table also presents the corresponding reasonable ranges, which I discuss 
25 
further in Section 5 below.  
26

18

3. COST OF CAPITAL PRINCIPLES AND APPROACH 
1 
A. Risk and the Cost of Capital 
2 
Q8: How is the “Cost of Capital” defined? 
3 
A8: The cost of capital is defined as the expected rate of return in capital markets on alternative 
4 
investments of equivalent risk.  Put differently, it is the rate of return investors require based 
5 
on the risk-return alternatives available in competitive capital markets.  The cost of capital 
6 
is a type of opportunity cost: it represents the rate of return that investors could expect to 
7 
earn elsewhere without bearing more risk. “Expected” is used in the statistical sense: the 
8 
mean of the distribution of possible outcomes. The terms “expect” and “expected,” as in the 
9 
definition of the cost of capital itself, refer to the probability-weighted average over all 
10 
possible outcomes. 
11 
The definition of the cost of capital recognizes a tradeoff between risk and return that can be 
12 
represented by the “security market risk-return line” or “Security Market Line” for short. 
13 
This line is depicted in Figure 2 below. The higher the risk, the higher the cost of capital 
14 
required. 
15 
Figure 2: The Security Market Line 
16 
 
17 


--- Page 9 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK  
VILLADSEN DIRECT 
 
 
Page 7 of 57 
Q9: What factors contribute to systematic risk for an equity investment? 
1 
A9: When estimating the cost of equity for a given asset or business venture, two categories of 
2 
risk are important. The first is business risk, which is the degree to which the cash flows 
3 
generated by the business (and its assets) vary in response to moves in the broader market. 
4 
In context of the CAPM, business risk can be quantified in terms of an “assets beta” or 
5 
“unlevered beta.” For a company with an assets beta of 1, the value of its enterprise will 
6 
increase (decrease) by 1% for a 1% increase (decline) in the market index. 
7 
The second category of risk relevant for an equity investment depends on how the business 
8 
enterprise is financed and is called financial risk. Section B below explains how financial 
9 
risk affects the systematic risk of equity. 
10 
Q10: What are the guiding standards that define a just and reasonable allowed rate of 
11 
return on rate-regulated utility investments? 
12 
A10: The seminal guidance on this topic was provided by the U.S. Supreme Court in the Hope 
13 
and Bluefield cases,4 which found that:  
14 
 The return to the equity owner should be commensurate with returns on investments 
15 
in other enterprises having corresponding risks;5  
16 
 The return should be reasonably sufficient to assure confidence in the financial 
17 
soundness of the utility; and  
18 
 The return should be adequate, under efficient and economical management for the 
19 
utility to maintain and support its credit and enable it to raise the money necessary 
20 
for the proper discharge of its public duties.6  
21 
 
4  Bluefield Water Works & Improvement Co. v. Public Service Com’n of West Virginia, 262 U.S.  679 (1923) 
(“Bluefield”), and Federal Power Com’n v. Hope Natural Gas Co., 320 U.S. 591 (1944) (“Hope”). 
5  Hope, 320 U.S. at 603. 
6  Bluefield, 262 U.S. at 680. 


--- Page 10 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK  
VILLADSEN DIRECT 
 
 
Page 8 of 57 
Q11: How does the standard for just and reasonable rate of return relate to the cost of 
1 
capital? 
2 
A11: The first component of the Hope and Bluefield standard, as articulated above, is directly 
3 
aligned with the financial concept of the opportunity cost of capital.7  The cost of capital is 
4 
the rate of return investors can expect to earn in capital markets on alternative investments 
5 
of equivalent risk.8 
6 
By investing in a regulated utility asset, investors are tying up some capital in that 
7 
investment, thereby foregoing alternative investment opportunities. Hence, the investors are 
8 
incurring an “opportunity cost” equal to the returns available on those alternative 
9 
investments. The allowed return on equity needs to be at least as high as the expected return 
10 
offered by alternative investments of equivalent risk or investors will choose these 
11 
alternatives instead.  If it is not, the utility’s ability to raise capital and fund its operations 
12 
will be negatively impacted. This is a fundamental concept in cost of capital proceedings for 
13 
regulated utilities such as CECONY. 
14 
Q12: Please summarize how you considered risk when estimating the cost of capital. 
15 
A12: To evaluate comparable business risk, I looked to a proxy group of regulated electric, natural 
16 
gas, and water utilities. The electric, natural gas, and water utilities I consider have a high 
17 
proportion of regulated assets and revenue with the majority having more than 80 percent of 
18 
assets subject to regulation. My recommendation focuses on the electric utilities group as 
19 
the Commission in the past has referenced an electric group for electric and gas utilities in 
20 
New York.9 Additionally, they all have a network of assets that are used to serve end-use 
21 
customers and they are capital intensive (meaning that each dollar in revenue requires 
22 
substantial investment in fixed assets).  Further (as explained in Section B below), I analyzed 
23 
 
7  A formal link between the opportunity cost of capital as defined by financial economics and the proper expected 
rate of return for utilities was developed by Stewart C. Myers, “Application of Finance Theory to Public Utility 
Rate Cases,” Bell Journal of Economics & Management Science 3:58-97 (1972). 
8 The opportunity cost of capital is also referred to as simply the “cost of capital,” and can be equivalently described 
in terms of the “required return” needed to attract investment in a particular security or other asset (i.e., the level 
of expected return at which investors will find that asset at least as attractive as an alternative investment).    
9 See, for example, State of New York Public Service Commission, “Staff Finance Panel Testimony”, Case Nos. 18-
E-0067, 18-G-0068, May 2018 as well as 19-E-0065 and 19-G-0066, May 2019. 


--- Page 11 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK  
VILLADSEN DIRECT 
 
 
Page 9 of 57 
and adjusted for differences in financial risk due to different levels of financial leverage 
1 
among the proxy companies and between the capital structures of the proxy companies and 
2 
the regulatory capital structure that will be applied to CECONY for ratemaking purposes. 
3 
To determine where in the estimated range of CECONY’s ROE reasonably falls, I compared 
4 
the business risk of CECONY to that of the proxy group companies. 
5 
B. Financial Risk and the Cost of Equity 
6 
Q13: How does capital structure affect the cost of equity? 
7 
A13: Debt holders in a company have a fixed claim on the assets of the company and are paid 
8 
prior to the company’s owners (equity holders), who hold the inherently variable residual 
9 
claim on the company’s operating cash flows. Because equity holders only receive the profit 
10 
that is left over after the fixed debt payments are made, higher degrees of debt in the capital 
11 
structure amplify the variability in the expected rate of return earned by equity-holders. This 
12 
phenomenon of debt resulting in financial leverage for equity holders means that, all else 
13 
equal, a greater proportion of debt in the capital structure increases risk for equity holders, 
14 
causing them to require a higher rate of return on their equity investment, even for an 
15 
equivalent level of underlying business risk. 
16 
Q14: How do differences in financial leverage affect the estimation of the cost of equity? 
17 
A14: The DCF models and the CAPM rely on market data to estimate the cost of equity for the 
18 
proxy companies, so the results reflect the value of the capital that investors hold during the 
19 
estimation period (market values).   
20 
The authorized ROE is applied to the regulatory equity portion of CECONY’s rate base.  
21 
Because the cost of equity is measured using a group of proxy companies, it may well be that 
22 
these companies finance their operations with a different debt and equity proportion than the 
23 
proportion the Commission allows in CECONY’s rate base.  Specifically, the DCF models 
24 
(and the CAPM) measure the cost of equity using market data and consequently are measures 
25 
of the cost of equity using the proportion of debt and equity that is inherent in that data. 
26 
Therefore, I consider the impact of any difference between the financial risk inherent in those 
27 


--- Page 12 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK  
VILLADSEN DIRECT 
 
 
Page 10 of 57 
cost of equity estimates and the capital structure used to determine CECONY’s required 
1 
return on equity. 
2 
Differences in financial risk due to the different degree of financial leverage in CECONY’s 
3 
regulatory capital structure compared to the capital structures of the proxy companies mean 
4 
that the equity betas measured for the proxy companies must be adjusted before they can be 
5 
applied in determining CECONY’s return on equity. Similarly, the cost of equity measured 
6 
by applying the DCF models to the proxy companies’ market data requires adjustment if it 
7 
is to serve as an estimate of the appropriate allowed ROE for CECONY at the regulatory 
8 
capital structure the Commission grants. 
9 
Importantly, taking differences in financial leverage into account does not change the value 
10 
of the rate base. Rather, it acknowledges the fact that a higher degree of financial leverage 
11 
in the regulatory capital structure imposes a higher degree of financial risk for an equity 
12 
investment in CECONY’s rate base than is experienced by equity investors in the market-
13 
traded stock of the less leveraged proxy companies. 
14 
Q15: How specifically do you consider financial risk in your analysis using market data for 
15 
the proxy group companies? 
16 
A15: The impact of financial risk is taken into account in an analysis of cost of equity using 
17 
market-based models such as the DCF and CAPM in several ways.10 One way is to determine 
18 
the after-tax weighted-average cost of capital for the proxy group using the equity and debt 
19 
percentages as the weight assigned to the cost of equity and debt.  Financial theory holds 
20 
that for a given level of business risk, the weighted average cost of capital is constant over a 
21 
broad set of capital structures, i.e., the weighted average cost of capital is the same at, for 
22 
example, 55 and 45 percent equity, as the cost of equity increases as the percentage of equity 
23 
decreases.  I estimate the weighted cost of capital for each utility in the proxy group based 
24 
on that utility’s capital structure. I then evaluate the average weighted cost of capital across 
25 
the proxy group. Once the weighted cost of capital is determined for the proxy group, I can 
26 
 
10 The impact of financial leverage on the risk premium model needs to be considered separately as it uses 
regulatory data rather than market data, meaning that it is the differences in regulatory capital structures are 
relevant for this model. 


--- Page 13 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK  
VILLADSEN DIRECT 
 
 
Page 11 of 57 
then determine the cost of equity that is required at CECONY’s capital structure. This 
1 
approach assumes that the after-tax weighted average cost of capital is constant for a range 
2 
that spans the capital structures used to estimate the cost of equity and the regulatory capital 
3 
structure. 
4 
A second approach was developed by Professor Hamada, who estimated the cost of equity 
5 
using the CAPM and made comparisons between companies with different capital structures 
6 
using beta. Specifically, in the Hamada approach, I use the estimated beta to calculate what 
7 
beta would be associated with a 100 percent equity financed firm to obtain a so-called all-
8 
equity or assets beta and then re-lever the beta to determine the beta associated with the 
9 
regulatory capital structure. This requires an estimate of the systematic risk associated with 
10 
debt (i.e., the debt beta), which is usually quite small. In Exhibit BV-2, I set forth additional 
11 
technical details regarding the methods that can be used to account for financial risk when 
12 
estimating the cost of capital. 
13 
Q16: Can you provide a numerical illustration of how the cost of equity changes, all else 
14 
being equal, when the degree of leverage changes? 
15 
A16: Yes. I constructed a simple example below, where only the leverage of a company varies.  I 
16 
assumed the return on equity is 11.00 percent at a 50 percent equity capital structure and 
17 
determine the return on equity that would result in the same overall return if the percentage 
18 
of equity in the capital structure were reduced to 45 percent. 
19

VILLADSEN DIRECT 
 
 
Page 12 of 57 
Figure 3 illustrates how financial risk11 affects returns and the ROE.  The overall return 
1 
remains the same for Company A and B at $80.  But Company B with the lower equity share 
2 
and higher financial leverage must earn a higher percentage ROE in order to maintain the 
3 
same overall return.  This higher percentage allowed ROE represents the increased risk to 
4 
equity investors caused by the higher degree of leverage. Importantly, regardless of the 
5 
equity percentage, customers will pay $80 in capital costs – the only difference between the 
6 
two companies is how that $80 is sourced between equity and debt holders. 
7 
The principle illustrated in Figure 3 is an example of the first adjustment I performed to 
8 
account for differences in financial risk when conducting estimates of the cost of equity 
9 
applicable to CECONY. 
10 
Q17: Does this approach apply to the risk premium analysis? 
11 
A17: Yes, to the extent that there are differences between the capital structures of the companies 
12 
used to determine the benchmark ROE and CECONY, I need to consider whether I am 
13 
comparing apples to apples. However, because the allowed ROE usually is applied to book 
14 
value capital structures, it is the book value capital structure that is relevant for the risk 
15 
premium method.  Further, the average book value capital structure for electric and natural 
16 
gas utilities for which I have allowed ROE data for, the past has been close to that of 
17 
CECONY, I do not need to make any adjustments to the estimated ROE.  I note, however 
18 
that for 2021, the average and median allowed equity percentage was 48.5% and 49.8%, 
19 
respectively for electric utilities and 49.1% and 50.0%, respectively, for natural gas 
20 
utilities.12 
21 
C. Approach to Estimating the Cost of Equity 
22 
Q18: Please describe your approach for determining the cost of equity for CECONY. 
23 
 
11 Financial risk is risk that a company has due to its capital structure; specifically, the higher a company’s debt, the 
larger the financial risk. 
12  S&P Global Market Intelligence, “Rate Case History” Online version as of October 31, 2021.  


--- Page 15 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK  
VILLADSEN DIRECT 
 
 
Page 13 of 57 
A18: As stated above, the standard for establishing a fair rate of return on equity requires that a 
1 
regulated utility be allowed to earn a return equivalent to what an investor could expect to 
2 
earn on an alternative investment of equivalent risk. Therefore, my approach to estimating 
3 
the cost of equity for CECONY focuses on measuring the expected returns required by 
4 
investors to invest in companies that face business and financial risks comparable to those 
5 
faced by CECONY. Because certain models require market data, my consideration of 
6 
comparable companies is restricted to those that have publicly traded stock. To this end, I 
7 
have selected two proxy groups consisting of publicly traded companies. The first proxy 
8 
group consists of companies providing primarily regulated electric utility services and the 
9 
second group consists of regulated natural gas distribution utilities.13 I consider the electric 
10 
utility, natural gas distribution, and the full samples when deriving estimates of the 
11 
representative cost of equity according to standard financial models, including two versions 
12 
of the DCF.   
13 
I also perform analyses of historical allowed ROEs for electric and natural gas utilities in 
14 
relation to prevailing risk-free interest rates at the time the ROE was authorized, and use the 
15 
implied allowed risk-premium relationship to estimate a utility cost of equity consistent with 
16 
current economic conditions. The results of this implied risk premium analysis (sometimes 
17 
referred to herein as the “Risk Premium” model) are an additional consideration that supports 
18 
my recommendation and serves as a check on the reasonableness of my market-based results. 
19 
Q19: How do your approach and models compare with those traditionally employed by the 
20 
Staff of the New York State Department of Public Service (“Staff”)?  
21 
A19: As exemplified in recent orders and Commission Staff Testimony regarding the Company’s 
22 
ROE,14  the Commission’s Generic Finance Methodology is broadly similar to, but also has 
23 
important differences from, my approach. 
24 
 
13 In addition to the electric sample, I consider a natural gas distribution utility sample (because CECONY is also a 
natural gas distribution utility).  
14 See, for example, State of New York Public Service Commission, “Order Adopting Terms of Joint Proposal and 
Establishing Electric and Gas Rate Plans,” Cases 19-E-0065, 19-G-0066, 18-E-0067, 18-G-0068 as well as the 
“Staff Finance Panel Testimony” in the same proceedings. 


--- Page 16 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK  
VILLADSEN DIRECT 
 
 
Page 14 of 57 
The market-based DCF and CAPM estimation techniques I rely on align with the 
1 
Commission’s historical reliance on both DCF and CAPM results to inform its allowed ROE 
2 
determinations. Of note, Staff has consistently implemented a “zero-beta” version of the 
3 
CAPM,15 which is conceptually and methodologically aligned with the version of the 
4 
empirical CAPM (i.e., ECAPM) that I implement. 
5 
While Staff and I both derive estimates from the DCF and CAPM, there are differences in 
6 
how we select inputs to implement the models. For example, Staff’s approach to the DCF 
7 
attempts to infer a “sustainable growth” rate based on Value Line forecasts of return on book 
8 
equity and retention ratio, whereas I implement both single- and multi-stage DCF models 
9 
based directly on forecasts (including by Value Line) of growth in earnings available for 
10 
distribution to investors.  The sustainable growth rate approach has two problems: (i) to my 
11 
knowledge, there is only one source of forecasted growth (Value Line) and (ii) Value Line’s 
12 
forecasted return on equity and the results obtained from using Value Line’s sustainable 
13 
growth is often inconsistent.  It is preferable to use growth rates from multiple sources – 
14 
IBES growth rates are based on forecasts from many financial analysts. Further, I believe 
15 
considering the results of both single and multi-stage models is appropriate in light of current 
16 
market conditions and their impact on dividend yields. 
17 
For the CAPM, Staff recently averaged the 10-year and 30-year government bond yield over 
18 
the most recent three months to obtain an estimate on the risk-free rate,16 whereas I look at 
19 
forecasts of the Treasury yield to capture investor expectations for the risk-free rate of return 
20 
during the period rates set in this proceeding will be in effect. While currently prevailing 
21 
yields are somewhat lower than the forecasted yield I use, the reverse is true of the market 
22 
risk premium (“MRP”) estimates traditionally relied on by Staff, which are significantly 
23 
higher than the estimate I employ, which (as discussed below) is supported by both historical 
24 
and forward-looking evidence.  Importantly, the forecasted MRP provides information about 
25 
the return investors expect going forward and which may differ from the historical average.  
26 
As discussed below, authors from the Federal Reserve of New York found that following the 
27 
 
15 “Staff Finance Panel Testimony” in Cases 19-E-0065 & 19-G-0066, p. 141. 
16 “Staff Finance Panel Testimony” in Cases 19-E-0065 & 19-G-0066, p. 95. 


--- Page 17 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK  
VILLADSEN DIRECT 
 
 
Page 15 of 57 
financial crisis of 2008-09, the return investors expect increased dramatically and that the 
1 
effect lingered for serveral years.  Given the return on equity determined in this proceeding 
2 
is expected to be in effect going forward and given that the economy in 2020-21 again has 
3 
seen substantial disruption, it is reasonable to consider the possibility of an effect similar to 
4 
that of 2008-09; i.e., that the return investors require in the current economic environment is 
5 
elevated. 
6 
Importantly, as discussed in Section 3.B, my CAPM and DCF analyses employ standard 
7 
finance techniques to adjust explicitly for differences in financial leverage between the proxy 
8 
group companies and the Company’s requested regulatory capital structure. The fact that 
9 
Staff’s typical approach does not take financial risk into account by using the standard 
10 
adjustment techniques means that Staff’s analysis misses an important step in estimating the 
11 
opportunity cost of capital commensurate with an investment of equivalent risk.17 
12 
Finally, in contrast to Staff’s practice, I do not believe it is appropriate to place fixed primary 
13 
emphasis on one model in deriving a recommended allowed ROE. Whereas the Commission 
14 
has traditionally placed 2/3 weight on the DCF and 1/3 on the CAPM, I consider the ranges 
15 
of results produced by the models I employ: two versions of the CAPM, two versions of the 
16 
DCF, and the implied Risk Premium method.  The reason I believe it is important to consider 
17 
the range is that I prefer to focus on the tendency of the data rather than a weighted average 
18 
of results for two models. Sprecifically, at any given point in time one of the relied upon 
19 
models may produce less reliable results.  For example, if a large number of companies have 
20 
only a single growth forecast of if it is dated, then the DCF methodology become unreliable.  
21 
As the U.S. economy continue to be substantially impacted by the ongoing COVID-19 
22 
pandemic and utilities’ systemic (market) risk have been impacted differently than, for 
23 
example, the S&P 500,18 it is not clear that a weighting that was derived years ago still is the 
24 
best method to determine the ROE that CECONY should be allowed to earn.  
25 
 
17 I am not aware of any MBA finance textbooks that do not discuss methods to account for financial risk. 
18 See Section 4.C below for details. 


--- Page 18 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK  
VILLADSEN DIRECT 
 
 
Page 16 of 57 
Q20: Why do you believe your approach to considering ranges of estimates derived from 
1 
multiple versions of both the DCF and CAPM, and also relying on an implied Risk 
2 
Premium analysis, is justified? 
3 
A20: There is no one perfect model for estimating the cost of equity, and the various models and 
4 
estimation approaches I employ each have different strengths and sensitivities. For example, 
5 
the CAPM relies on an explicit measurement of systematic risk (beta) for which the cost of 
6 
equity capital must compensate investors, but this parameter must be measured using 
7 
historical data,19 and thus changes more slowly in response to changes in industry risk 
8 
characteristics. Conversely, the DCF models incorporate current market prices and the most 
9 
recent dividends, enabling them to capture shifts over time. However, this also makes the 
10 
DCF sensitive to short-term market phenomena that may or may not be representative of the 
11 
capital market conditions and required investor returns that will prevail during the time 
12 
CECONY’s electric and gas rates are in effect. In contrast to both the CAPM and DCF 
13 
models, the implied risk premium analysis focuses directly on the relationship of allowed 
14 
returns for regulated utility companies to observable rates of return (i.e., bond yields) 
15 
reflective of contemporaneous capital market conditions. 
16 
4. CAPITAL MARKET CONDITIONS AND THE COST OF CAPITAL 
17 
Q21: What do you cover in this section? 
18 
A21: In this section, I address recent changes in capital market conditions, the increased volatility 
19 
in equity and debt markets, and how these factors affect the cost of equity and its estimation. 
20 
Specifically, I address (i) interest rate developments; (ii) investors perception of the market 
21 
risk premium, and (iii) inflation expectations.20 
22 
Q22: Why do you discuss capital market conditions in a testimony aimed at determining 
23 
CECONY’s ROE? 
24 
 
19 I note that Value Line applies an empirical adjustment (the Blume adjustment) that converts the beta derived from 
historical return data into a better indicator of forward-looking systematic risk (i.e., a better predictor of beta going 
forward). 
20  In past testimony, I have discussed utility credit spreads but not inflation.  Utility credit spreads are currently only 
modestly elevated, so I do not attempt to account for this. However, inflation expectations have become a source 
of substantial concern in recent months and therefore merit consideration.

Page 17 of 57 
A22: Capital market conditions are important to cost of equity estimation methodologies and can 
1 
affect the inputs to the cost of equity models. Inputs to the DCF models are affected by the 
2 
economy in general as economic growth will affect growth rates and utility stock prices. 
3 
Consequently, the capital market developments affect the growth rates, dividend yield, and 
4 
the assessment of estimates’ reasonableness. 
5 
Furthermore, the risk-free rate is an input to the risk premium model and CAPM, so that 
6 
recent and expected developments in government bond yields are important to assess the 
7 
validity of any measure of the risk-free rate. Similarly, the MRP is an input to the CAPM, so 
8 
factors that affect the MRP (e.g., volatility and changes in investors’ risk perceptions) are 
9 
vital for accurate determination of the ROE.  Lastly, inflation has recently been substantially 
10 
above what the U.S. has become accustomed to and is expected to be above the Federal 
11 
Reserve’s two percent target for a period.21  Uncertainty about inflation expectations create 
12 
uncertainty about the cost of capital as well as other aspects of utilities’ costs. 
13 
Q23: Can you provide a summary of recent events, which have impacted capital market 
14 
conditions? 
15 
A23: Capital markets have seen historic changes since CECONY’s last rate case settlement was 
16 
approved in January 2020.22 Notably, COVID-19 has emerged as a substantial health risk 
17 
and responses aimed at containing the virus or stimulating the economy have had a profound 
18 
impact on financial markets.   
19 
In the spring of 2020, a number of states, including New York, implemented emergency 
20 
measures such as work-from-home mandates and other business restrictions.  As a result, 
21 
economic activity slowed and in March 2020, the S&P 500 dropped by 30%.23 
22 
Unemployment also increased as businesses were closed.  In turn, the Federal Reserve cut 
23 
its target range for the policy rate to 0-0.25 percent and announced unlimited quantitative 
24 
 
21  Blue Chip Economic Indicators, October 2021 expects 2022 inflation to be approximately 3.2%.

conditions.

easing and emergency liquidity programs to support financial markets.24  The Federal 
1

2

3

4

5

6

8

10 
Q24: What do you cover in the remainder of this section?  
11 
A24: First, I focus on expected developments in interest rates, which directly impact the cost of 
12 
equity as estimated by two standard models (the CAPM and risk premium model).  Interest 
13 
rates also may impact indirectly the DCF method as investors’ expectations concerning 
14 
interest rates may impact stock prices and growth.  Second, I discuss investor expectations 
15 
as to the market risk premium, i.e., the return over and above the risk-free rate that investors 
16 
require to hold equity.  This measure again affects the inputs to the financial models and the 
17 
interpretation of the results.  Third, I address the recent changes in electric utilities systematic 
18 
risk.  Fourth, I discuss growth and inflation expectations, which directly affect the DCF 
19 
model, as well the other models through the expected interest rate developments.

A. INTEREST RATES 
21 
Q25: How do interest rates affect the cost of equity? 
22 
A25: The current interest rate environment affects the cost of equity estimation in several ways. 
23 
Most directly, the CAPM takes as one of its inputs a measure of the risk-free rate.25 The 
24 
estimated cost of equity using the CAPM decreases (increases) by one percentage point when 
25 
 
24 U.S. Federal Reserve, “Federal Reserve Announces Extensive New Measures to Support the Economy,” Press

the risk-free rate decreases (increases) by one percentage point. Therefore, to the extent that 
1 
prevailing government yields are depressed due to economic uncertainties related to the 
2 
COVID-19 pandemic or the monetary policy responses, using current yields as the risk-free 
3 
rate would depress the CAPM estimate below what is representative of the forward-looking 
4 
cost of equity. Therefore, the allowed fair return on equity for CECONY should reflect the 
5 
future interest rate environment, specifically the environment at the time the rates being set 
6 
in this proceeding will be in effect. 
7 
Q26: What are the relevant developments regarding interest rates? 
8 
A26: Current interest rates on 10-year U.S. Government bonds remain low at 1.58 percent,26 
9

10 
behaviors by investors as well as the Federal Reserve’s expansion of its quantitative easing 
11 
programs. Interest rates on 10-year U.S. Government bonds were about 2.6 percent in 
12 
January 2019 (when Cases 19-E-0065 and 19-G-0066 were filed), 1.7 percent in October 
13

14 
Joint Proposal was approved by the Commission).27  As large parts of the economy began to 
15 
shut down in response to the pandemic, investors fled riskier assets for safer assets. This 
16 
demand for U.S. government bonds caused bond yields to decrease rapidly. On March 9, 
17 
2020, the entire U.S. yield curve fell below 100 basis points for the first time in history and 
18 
the 10-year U.S. government bond yield hit a record low of 0.339 percent.28  Since then, 
19 
long-term government bond yields have increased somewhat — the yield on 10-year U.S. 
20 
Government bonds as of October 2021 was 1.55 percent – well below the historical norm.29  
21 
Looking forward, treasury bonds are forecasted to increase, which is depicted in Figure 4

Q28: What is the Market Risk Premium (MRP)? 
14 
A28: In general, a risk premium is the amount of “excess” return—above the risk-free rate of 
15 
return—that investors require to compensate them for taking on risk. As illustrated in Figure

2Error! Reference source not found.  the riskier the investment, the larger the risk 
1 
premium investors will require. 
2 
The MRP is the risk premium associated with investing in the market as a whole. Since the 
3 
so-called “market portfolio” embodies the maximum possible degree of diversification for 
4 
investors,36 the MRP is a highly relevant benchmark indicating the level of risk compensation 
5 
demanded by capital market participants. It is also a direct input necessary to estimating the 
6 
cost of equity using the CAPM and other risk-positioning models. 
7 
Q29: Please explain the current evidence related to the MRP. 
8 
A29: The heightened volatility in the market has increased the premium that investors require to 
9 
hold risky assets, especially when measured utilizing forward-looking methodologies that 
10 
estimate expected market returns with reference to current dividend yields. Bloomberg’s 
11 
forward-looking estimate of the MRP for the U.S. increased to as high as 9.84 percent in 
12 
March 2020 and remained high at 8.39 percent as of October 2021.37 The current forward 
13 
market risk premium investors require to hold risky assets remains substantially elevated 
14 
compared to both the long-term historical premium and premiums required during pre-
15 
pandemic years since capital markets’ slow recovery following the financial crisis.  
16 
Academic research has shown that market disruptions lead to a prolonged MRP impact as 
17 
discussed below. 
18 
 
36  In finance theory, the “market portfolio” describes a value-weighted combination of all risky investment assets 
(e.g., stocks, bonds, real estate) that can be purchased in markets. In practice, academics and financial analysts 
nearly always use a broad-based stock market index, such as the S&P 500, to represent the overall market.

Q30: Are higher risk premiums relevant given that treasuries are near historic lows? 
3 
A30: Yes—this is highly relevant for cost of equity estimation, as current risk-free rates are 
4 
extremely low. As shown in Figure 7 above, the MRP has increased as the risk-free rate 
5 
declined. Both academic and industry analyses have found that the allowed risk premium 
6 
over the risk-free rate is inversely related to the risk-free rate.  For example, Villadsen, et al. 
7 
(2017) found that the allowed risk premium increases by approximately 0.44 percent for 
8 
each 1 percent decline in the risk-free rate for the period 1990 to 2015.38 Morin finds that the 
9 
risk premium increases by 0.52 percent for each 1 percent decline in the risk-free rate.39 This 
10 
is consistent with Figure 7 above, which focuses on the risk premium for the forward-looking 
11 
market risk premium calculated by Bloomberg. According to Bloomberg, the MRP is 
12 
currently 7.89 percent over the 20-year Treasury bond,40 which is higher than the historical 
13

average MRP of about 7.25 percent.  It is also an increase over the forward-looking MRPs 
1 
measured at the end of 2019 (pre-COVID) of 6.48 percent and the approximately 7 percent 
2 
I reported in the last CECONY rate cases.41 
3 
Q31: Is there evidence that the MRP will remain elevated going forward? 
4 
A31: Yes. In 2015, Duarte and Rose of the Federal Reserve of New York performed a study that 
5 
aggregated the results of many models of the required MRP in the United States and tracked 
6 
them over time.42 This analysis found a very high MRP after the financial crisis, relative to 
7 
time periods prior the crisis. 
8 
The authors estimated the MRP that resulted from a range of models each year from 1960 
9 
through the time of their study. The authors then reported the average, as well as the first 
10 
principal component of the results.43 The authors found that the models used to determine 
11 
the risk premium were converging to provide comparable estimates and that the average 
12 
annual estimate of the MRP had reached an all-time high in 2012-2013. (Error! Reference 
13 
source not found. below is a copy of the summary chart from Duarte and Rosa’s 2015 
14 
paper). These directional trends identified by Duarte and Rosa are reasonably consistent with 
15 
those observed from Bloomberg and they further support the proposition that the elevation 
16 
of the MRP over its historical pre-crisis levels was a persistent feature of capital markets in 
17 
the time following the financial crisis. Specifically, the financial crisis saw high volatility 
18 
and a flight to quality – similar to conditions seen in 2020 in response to the COVID-19 
19 
pandemic.  Therefore, it is reasonable to expect that the current MRP will remain elevated 
20 
compared to historical levels, especially given the uncertainty related to the extent of 
21 
economic and financial impacts from the COVID-19 pandemic and the historically low 
22 
interest rates. 
23 
 
41 Id. 
42 Fernando Durate and Carlo Rosa, “The Equity Risk Premium: A Review of Models,” Federal Reserve Bank of 
New York, December 2015 (“Duarte and Rosa, 2015”) 
 https://www.newyorkfed.org/research/staff_reports/sr714.html. 
43 Duarte and Rosa emphasize the “first principal component” of the 20 models. This means that the authors used 
statistics to compute the weighted average combination of the models that captures the variability among the 20 
models over time.

ONE-YEAR AHEAD MRP AND CROSS-SECTIONAL MEAN OF MODELS 
2 
 
3 
Q32: Please summarize how the economic developments discussed above have affected the 
4 
return on equity and debt that investors require. 
5 
A32: Utilities rely on investors in capital markets to provide funding to support their capital 
6 
expenditure programs and efficient business operations. Investors consider the risk-return 
7 
tradeoff in choosing how to allocate their capital among different investment opportunities. 
8 
It is therefore important to consider how investors view the current economic conditions, 
9 
including the plausible developments in the risk-free rate and the growth in the U.S. GDP. 
10 
These investors have been affected by the recent market volatility, so there are reasons to 
11 
believe that their risk aversion remains and will remain elevated relative to pre-COVID-19 
12 
levels and therefore require will require a higher return. As CECONY is expected to be 
13 
compensated as a utility on the equity component of its rate base, the same factors would 
14 
affect CECONY’s equity. 
15 
C. UTILITIES SYSTEMATIC RISK 
16 
Q33: Are there indications that electric utilities systematic (non-diversifiable) risk has 
17 
changed? 
18 
A33: Yes.  The relative risk of electric utilities such as CECONY has increased as demonstrated 
19 
by the substantial increase in the systematic (non-diversifiable) risk (measured by beta) with 
20 
electric utilities moving closer to exhibiting risks similar to the market in general as shown 
21 
in Figure 9 and Figure 10 below.  Specifically, the figures below depict the development in 
22 


--- Page 29 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK  
VILLADSEN DIRECT 
 
 
Page 27 of 57 
Value Line betas since 2013 (Figure 9) and estimated 3-year weekly betas (Figure 10).44 
1 
Whether I use the Value Line reported beta or estimate betas, the result is clear – the 
2 
systematic risk of electric utilities has increased.  Today’s beta of approximately 0.91 for 
3 
electric utilities is higher than in the past.45  At the time of the last cost of capital proceeding 
4 
for CECONY, I found an average Value Line beta of 0.58 and my estimated 3-year beta as 
5 
of year-end 2019 was below 0.51.  The increase in beta is material because a higher beta, all 
6 
else equal, will lead to a higher cost of equity regardless of the yield on government bonds.  
7 
Specifically, the risk premium investors require to hold electric utility stock today is higher 
8 
than at the time of the last cost of capital proceeding.  The change in the risk-free rate 
9 
therefore cannot in and of itself be used to assess the level of or the directional change in the 
10 
cost of equity.  Simply put, other factors influence the cost of equity (e.g., beta and market 
11 
risk premia) and these factors have moved in a direction that increases the cost of equity.   
12 
 
13

Looking to a more recent measure of systematic risk, Figure 10 shows rolling three-year

Q34: What conclusions do you draw from the discussion above regarding beta? 
7 
A34: Because the utility-specific risk premium is the multiple of the market risk premium and the 
8 
utility beta, the dramatic increase in beta combined with an increase in the MRP has resulted 
9 
in a substantially higher utility-specific risk premium.  Put simply, the return over and above 
10 
the risk-free rate that utility investors require has increased and it has increased by more than 
11 
the risk-free rate has declined since the last CECONY rate cases. 
12

14

A35: The return on equity that is being determined now is expected to be applicable in future years 
1 
(e.g., 2023 and beyond), so CECONY will be exposed to economic developments over the 
2 
period for which rates are set.  Because the allowed return on equity is a nominal return, it 
3 
includes today’s inflation, but going forward the inflation could readily change.  Historically, 
4 
inflation has impacted not only product prices but also the cost of capital.48 
5 
Q36: What are recent indicators of the growth and inflation for the US economy? 
6 
A36: Recent surveys by economists, such as the BCEI survey, indicate that U.S. real GDP will 
7 
increase by 4.1% in 2022 and 2.5% in 2023 for a nominal GDP at about 6%, and a bit below 
8 
5%, respectively.49 In August 2020, the U.S. Federal Reserve announced a policy change 
9 
whereby it would target inflation of 2% on average, noting that the Federal Reserve would 
10 
hold overnight borrowing interest rates lower for longer.50  The Federal Reserve has 
11 
remained cautious about the pace and extent of the ongoing recovery. Until recently, the 
12 
Federal Reserve indicated that inflation was transitory.  For example, in the July 2021 
13 
meeting of the FOMC, the FOMC concluded51 
14 
The sectors most adversely affected by the pandemic have shown improvement 
15 
but have not fully recovered. Inflation has risen, largely reflecting transitory 
16 
factors. 
17 
And 
18 
The path of the economy continues to depend on the course of the virus. 
19 
More recently, however, the Federal Reserve as well as other government officials have 
20 
indicated that inflation may last a bit longer.  For example, Mr. Powell’s recently stated that 
21 
he expects inflation will linger “well into next year”52 and the December 2021 meeting of 
22

Q37: How do these events impact the cost of equity estimation for CECONY? 
1 
A37: The expected interest rate, market risk premium and GDP growth rate directly impact the 
2 
cost of equity as determined by the CAPM and DCF model.  Additionally, inflation 
3 
expectations and the broader economic conditions affect investors’ return and growth 
4 
expectations.  Thus, the factors discussed above impact the cost of equity, which inherently 
5 
is a forward-looking concept. 
6 
5. ESTIMATING THE COST OF EQUITY 
7 
A. Proxy Group Selection 
8 
Q38: How do you identify proxy companies of comparable business risk to CECONY? 
9 
A38: CECONY is primarily engaged in the regulated electric and natural gas distribution business. 
10 
The business risk associated with these endeavors depends on many factors, including the 
11 
specific characteristics of the service territory and regulatory environment in which the 
12 
provider of these services operates. Consequently, it is not possible to identify publicly 
13 
traded proxy companies that replicate every aspect of CECONY’s risk profile. However, 
14 
selecting companies with business operations concentrated in regulated industries or having 
15 
similar lines of business and/or business environments is an appropriate starting point for 
16 
selecting one or more proxy groups of comparable risk to CECONY.  As a second step, I 
17 
must evaluate CECONY, service-territory or New York-specific risks so that the Company’s 
18 
ROE is placed appropriately relative to the sample companies. 
19 
To this end, I have selected a sample of electric and natural gas companies.  Jointly these 
20 
companies comprise the “Full Sample.” I also report results for the electric utilities that are 
21 
included in the Full Sample and refer to that sample as the “Electric Sample.” I similarly 
22 
report the results for natural gas utilities that are included in the full sample and refer to that 
23 
as the “Natural Gas Sample.” The proxy companies are similar to CECONY in that they are 
24 
rate regulated by state utility commissions, provide customers a product through a network 
25 
of assets, and rely on substantial capital to provide service; i.e., they are capital intensive as 
26 
is CECONY. Additionally, all regulated utilities are subject to conservation initiatives, many 
27 


--- Page 35 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK  
VILLADSEN DIRECT 
 
 
Page 33 of 57 
have recently faced moratoriums on shut-offs,65 and consumption patterns have changed 
1 
towards residential use during the COVID-19 pandemic period.66   
2 
It is important that a proxy group used to assess the cost of equity for CECONY (absent of 
3 
any unique New York or Company characteristics) is regulated, because regulation tends to 
4 
place substantial requirements and also protections on the companies.  I also believe the 
5 
physical characteristics of the industry – e.g., network, capital intensive, serving different 
6 
customer groups (residential, commercial, industrial) – is a characteristic of CECONY and 
7 
of the selected electric and natural gas distribution utilities. The network characteristic 
8 
implies that assets cannot readily be employed in a different capacity, capital intensity affects 
9 
the operating risks through the split between fixed and variable costs, and the customer 
10 
composition affects the demand risk.  For example, many electric and natural gas utilities 
11 
face declining per-customer demand due to conservation and regulation (legislation or 
12 
voluntary commitments). 
13 
Q39: Please summarize how you selected the Electric and Natural Gas samples? 
14 
A39: To identify companies suitable for inclusion in the Full Sample, I started with the universe 
15 
of publicly traded companies in the electric and natural gas utility industry as identified by 
16 
Value Line Investment Analyzer (“Value Line”). Next, I reviewed business descriptions and 
17 
financial reports of these companies and eliminated companies that had less than 50 percent 
18 
of their assets dedicated to regulated utility activities in their industry.67 
19 
With this group of companies, I applied further screening criteria to eliminate companies that 
20 
have had recent significant events that could affect the market data necessary to perform cost 
21 
of capital estimation. Specifically, I identified companies that have cut their dividends or 
22 
engaged in substantial merger and acquisition (“M&A”) activities over the relevant 
23 
 
65  Lillian Federico, “Bans on utility shut-offs during COVID-19 pandemic challenge regulators,” S&P Market 
Intelligence, August 28, 2020, https://www.spglobal.com/marketintelligence/en/news-insights/blog/bans-on-
utility-shut-offs-during-covid19-pandemic-challenge-regulators. 
66 Darren Sweeney, “Warm weather, residential power sales help utilities offset demand declines,” S&P Market 
Intelligence, August 4, 2020, https://www.spglobal.com/marketintelligence/en/news-insights/latest-news-
headlines/warm-weather-residential-power-sales-help-utilities-offset-demand-declines-59727866 
67 For electric utilities, I rely on Edison Electric Institute (EEI), 2019 Financial Review. This report gives industry 
financial information as well as a percentage of regulated assets for each of the companies.

Page 34 of 57 
estimation window.68 I eliminated companies with such dividend cuts because the 
1 
announcement of a cut may produce disturbances in the stock prices and growth rate 
2 
expectations in addition to potentially being a signal of financial distress. I eliminated 
3 
companies with significant M&A activities because such events typically affect a company’s 
4 
stock price in ways that are not representative of how investors perceive its business and 
5 
financial risk characteristics. For example, a utility’s stock price will commonly jump upon 
6 
the announcement of an acquisition to match the acquirer’s bid. 
7 
Further, I require companies have an investment grade credit rating69 and more than $300 
8 
million in market capitalization to avoid microcap companies. A final, and fundamental, 
9 
requirement is that the proxy companies have the necessary data available for estimation. 
10 
Q40: What are the characteristics of the Electric and Natural Gas Utility samples? 
11 
A40: I calculate my results for both the electric proxy group and for the natural gas utility proxy 
12 
group. The proxy group(s) are comprised of electric and natural gas utilities whose primary 
13 
source of revenues and majority of assets are subject to regulation. The final proxy group 
14 
consists of the 27 electric utilities and 8 natural gas utilities listed in Figure 11 and Figure 
15 
12 below, respectively. 
16 
The figures below report the proxy companies’ annual revenues for the most recent four 
17 
quarters as of 3Q 2021 and also report the market capitalization, credit rating, beta and 
18 
growth rate.  The annual revenue, as well as the market cap, was obtained from Bloomberg. 
19 
The credit rating is reported by Bloomberg.70 The growth rate estimate is a weighted average 
20 
between estimates from Thomson Reuters and Value Line.  Betas were obtained from Value 
21 
Line. 
22 
 
68 As described below, the CAPM requires five years of historical data, while the DCF relies on current market data.

average credit rating of the rest of the proxy group.   
70 In cases where a company does not have an S&P rating from Bloomberg, Moody’s rating was obtained from

B. The CAPM Based Cost of Equity Estimates 
1 
Q43: Please briefly explain the CAPM. 
2 
A43: CAPM assumes the collective investment decisions of investors in capital markets will result 
3 
in equilibrium prices for all risky assets such that the returns investors expect to receive on 
4 
their investments are commensurate with the risk of those assets relative to the market as a 
5 
whole.  The CAPM posits a risk-return relationship known as the Security Market Line (see 
6 
Figure 2 in Section 3), in which the required expected return on an asset (above the risk-free 
7 
return) is proportional to that asset’s relative risk as measured by that asset’s beta. 
8 
More precisely, the CAPM states that the cost of capital for an investment, S (e.g., a 
9 
particular common stock), is determined by the risk-free rate plus the stock’s systematic risk 
10 
(as measured by beta) multiplied by the market risk premium. Mathematically, the 
11 
relationship is given by the following equation: 
12 
  
𝒓𝒔ൌ𝒓𝒇൅𝜷𝒔ൈ𝑴𝑹𝑷 
 
 
 
 
 
(1) 
13 
 𝒓𝑺 is the cost of capital for investment S; 
14 
 𝒓𝒇 is the risk-free interest rate; 
15 
 𝜷𝑺 is the beta risk measure for the investment S; and 
16 
 𝑴𝑹𝑷 is the market equity risk premium. 
17 
The CAPM is a “risk-positioning model,” which operates on the principle (corroborated by 
18 
empirical data) that investors price risky securities to offer a higher expected rate of return 
19 
than safe securities.  It says that an investment, whose returns do not vary relative to market 
20 
returns, should receive the risk-free interest rate (that is the return on a zero-risk security, the 
21 
y-axis intercept in Figure 2), whereas investments of the same risk as the overall market (i.e., 
22 
those that by definition have average systematic market risk) are priced so as to expect to 
23 
return the risk-free rate plus the MRP.  Further, it says that the risk premium of a security 
24 
over the risk-free rate equals the product of the beta of that security and the MRP.73 
25 
 
73 Of note, empirical evidence indicates that the CAPM estimates are too low for smaller companies such as CECONY.  
For example, Duff & Phelps provide an adder for companies by size.  While I do not provide a size adder, regulators 
such as the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission do add a premium to smaller companies cost of equity. 


--- Page 40 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK  
VILLADSEN DIRECT 
 
 
Page 38 of 57 
1. Inputs to the CAPM 
1 
Q44: What inputs does your implementation of the CAPM require? 
2 
A44: As demonstrated by equation (1), estimating the cost of equity for a given company requires 
3 
a measure of the risk-free rate of interest and the MRP, as well as a measure of the stock’s 
4 
beta. There are several choices and sources of data that inform the selection of these inputs. 
5 
I discuss these issues below. (Additional technical detail, along with a discussion of the 
6 
finance theory underlying the CAPM is provided in Exhibit BV-2). 
7 
Q45: What value did you use for the risk-free rate of interest? 
8 
A45: I use the yield on a 20-year U.S. Treasury bond as the risk-free rate for purposes of my 
9 
analysis. Recognizing the fact that the cost of capital set in this proceeding will be in effect 
10 
starting 2022 and through 2024, I rely on a forecast of the average Government bond yields 
11 
in the rate period. In October 2021, the BCEI survey estimated the average 10-year U.S. 
12 
Treasury bond yields between 2022 and 2024 will be 2.23%.74 I then adjust this value 
13 
upwards by 50 basis points to reflect the historical maturity premium for the 20-year U.S. 
14 
Treasury bond yield over the 10-year U.S. Treasury bond yield.75 This gives me a risk-free 
15 
rate of 2.73%. 
16 
Q46: What value did you use for the MRP? 
17 
A46: Like the cost of capital itself, the MRP is a forward-looking concept.  It is by definition the 
18 
premium above the risk-free interest rate that investors can expect to earn by investing in a 
19 
value-weighted portfolio of all risky investments in the market.  The premium is not directly 
20 
observable. Rather, it must be inferred or forecasted based on known market information.  
21 
One commonly used method for estimating the MRP is to measure the historical average 
22 
premium of market returns over the income returns on government bonds a long historical 
23 
 
74 Wolters Kluwer Blue Chip Economic Indicators and PwC Analysis, Consensus Forecasts, October 2021, p. 14. 
75 This maturity premium is estimated by comparing the average excess yield on 20-year versus 10-year U.S. Treasury 
bonds over the period 1990-2020, using data from Bloomberg. 


--- Page 41 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK  
VILLADSEN DIRECT 
 
 
Page 39 of 57 
period.76 The average market risk premium from 1926 to the present (2020) is 7.25 percent.77  
1 
I use this value of the MRP along with a risk-free rate of 2.73% in one of my CAPM 
2 
scenarios.  
3 
However, investors may require a higher or lower risk premium, reflecting the investment 
4 
alternatives and aggregate level of risk aversion at any given time. As explained in Section 
5 
4, there is evidence that investors’ level of risk aversion is elevated relative to the time before 
6 
the COVID-19 pandemic and may remain elevated for some time, even after the pandemic. 
7 
In recognition of the evidence that forward-looking measures of expected market equity risk 
8 
premium are higher than the long-term historical average, I also perform a CAPM calculation 
9 
using Bloomberg’s forecasted MRP of 7.89 percent.78 
10 
Q47: Please summarize the parameters of the scenarios and variations you considered in 
11 
your CAPM and ECAPM analyses. 
12 
A47: The parameters are displayed in Figure 13 below. In my CAPM and ECAPM analyses, I 
13 
consider two sets of scenarios based on the empirical observation that the yield spread is 
14 
higher than normal as is the forecast MRP, as discussed above in Section 4. The increase 
15 
yield spreads could reflect the increase in MRP or downward pressure on the yield of 
16 
government bonds due to monetary policy and flight-to-quality behaviors. Therefore, I use 
17 
an unadjusted historic average MRP with the increased estimate of the risk-free rate in one 
18 
scenario; whereas, in the second scenario I use an unadjusted forecasted risk-free rate with 
19 
a higher estimate of the MRP. To be conservative, I do not simultaneously normalize the 
20 
risk-free rate and elevate the MRP. 
21 
Scenario 1 uses an unadjusted risk-free rate based on the forecasted 20-year U.S. Treasury 
22 
rate for 2021 of 2.73%. I pair this with the long-term average historic MRP of 7.25% as 
23 
estimated by Duff & Phelps.  
24 
 
76 The longest period for which Duff & Phelps reports data is 1926 to current.  Based on financial textbooks such as 
Ross, Westerfield and Jaffe, “Corporate Finance,” 10’th Edition, 2013, pp. 324-327, I use the longest period for 
which reliable estimates are available – in this case 1926 to 2020.  
77 Duff & Phelps, Ibbotson SBBI 2020 Valuation Yearbook 10-21.

Q48: What betas did you use for the companies in your proxy groups? 
5 
A48: I used Value Line betas, which are estimated using the most recent five years of weekly 
6 
historical returns data.80 The Value Line levered equity betas are reported in Figure 11 above. 
7 
Importantly, these betas—which are measured (by Value Line) using the market stock return 
8 
data of the proxy companies—reflect the level of financial risk inherent in the proxy 
9 
companies’ market value leverage ratios over the estimation period. Because CECONY’s 
10 
regulatory capital structure includes a substantially higher proportion of debt financing than 
11 
does the market data on the proxy companies used to estimate the ROE81, the financial risk 
12 
associated with an equity investment in CECONY’s rate base is correspondingly greater than 
13 
the financial risk borne by investors in the proxy companies’ publicly traded stock.  
14 
Importantly, the DCF model and the CAPM-based models use market data to estimate the 
15 
ROE, so that it is the market value capital structure that is the relevant comparison across 
16 
companies. As the risk premium model’s ROE estimates are based on book value capital 
17 
structures, the relevant comparison is across book value capital structures for that model. 
18

2. The Empirical CAPM 
4 
Q49: What other equity risk premium model do you use? 
5 
A49: Empirical research has long shown that the CAPM tends to overstate the actual sensitivity 
6 
of the cost of capital to beta: low-beta stocks tend to have higher risk premiums than 
7 
predicted by the CAPM and high-beta stocks tend to have lower risk premiums than 
8 
predicted.83 A number of variations on the original CAPM theory have been proposed to 
9 
explain this finding, but the observation itself can also be used to estimate the cost of capital 
10 
directly, using beta to measure relative risk by making a direct empirical adjustment to the 
11 
CAPM. 
12 
The second variation on the CAPM that I employ makes use of these empirical findings. It 
13 
estimates the cost of capital with the equation, 
14 
𝒓𝑺ൌ𝒓𝒇൅𝜶൅𝜷𝑺ൈሺ𝑴𝑹𝑷െ𝜶ሻ                         (2) 
15 
where 𝜶 is the “alpha” adjustment of the risk-return line, a constant, and the other symbols 
16 
are defined as for the CAPM (see equation (2) above). 
17 
I label this model the Empirical Capital Asset Pricing Model, or “ECAPM.”  The alpha 
18 
adjustment has the effect of increasing the intercept but reducing the slope of the Security 
19 
Market Line in Figure 2, which results in a Security Market Line that more closely matches 
20 
the results of empirical tests. This adjustment is portrayed in Figure 14 below. In other words, 
21 
 
82 The Technical Appendix (Exhibit BV-2) to this testimony provides a detailed description of the standard textbook 
formulas used to implement the “Hamada” technique for unlevering measured equity betas based on the proxy 
companies’ capital structures to calculate “asset betas” that measure the proxy companies’ business risk independent 
of the financial risk impact of differing capital structures. The proxy group average asset betas are then relevered at 
the target capital structure (i.e., CECONY’s regulatory capital structure), with the precise relevered beta depending 
on the specific version of the unlevering/relevering formula employed. 
83 See Figure A-2 in Exhibit BV-2 for references to relevant academic articles. 


--- Page 44 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK  
VILLADSEN DIRECT 
 
 
Page 42 of 57 
the ECAPM produces more accurate predictions of eventual realized risk premiums than 
1 
does the CAPM. 
2 
Figure 14: The Empirical Security Market Line 
3 
   
4 
Q50: Why do you use the ECAPM? 
5 
A50: Academic research finds that the CAPM has not generally performed well as an empirical 
6 
model. One of its shortcomings is directly addressed by the ECAPM, which recognizes the 
7 
consistent empirical observation that the CAPM underestimates the cost of capital for low 
8 
beta stocks. In other words, the ECAPM is based on recognizing that the actual observed 
9 
risk-return line is flatter and has a higher intercept than that predicted by the CAPM. The 
10 
alpha parameter (α) in the ECAPM adjusts for this fact, which has been established by 
11 
repeated empirical tests of the CAPM. In summary, these studies estimate alpha parameters 
12 
that range between 1%84 and 7.32%.85 I apply an alpha parameter of 1.5% in my application 
13 
 
84 Black, Fischer. Beta and Return. The Journal of Portfolio Management 20 (Fall): 8-18. 
85 Eugene F. Fama and Kenneth R. French. 1992. The Cross-Section of Expected Stock Returns. Journal of Finance 
47 (June): 427-465. 


--- Page 45 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK  
VILLADSEN DIRECT 
 
 
Page 43 of 57 
of the ECAPM. Exhibit BV-2 provides further discussion of the empirical findings that have 
1 
tested the CAPM and also provides documentation for the magnitude of the adjustment, . 
2

Q52: How do you interpret the results of your CAPM and ECAPM Analyses? 
1 
A52: The results in Figure 15 above range from 10.2% to 11.7% with the majority of the results 
2 
in the range of 10.25 to 11.25 percent.86 As I discussed above, the established academic 
3 
evidence indicates that the traditional CAPM tends to underestimate the cost of equity for 
4 
lower-than-average risk companies, such as the electric and natural gas utilities in Figure 11, 
5 
so the ECAPM results are more reliable and the CAPM results are conservative. I also note 
6 
that the CAPM may under-estimate the cost of equity for smaller companies such as 
7 
CECONY. As a result, I consider a reasonable range of 10.25 to 11.25 percent for both the 
8 
electric and the natural gas sample and the overall recommendation of 10.0 to 10.5 percent 
9 
is conservative. 
10 
C. DCF Based Estimates 
11 
Q53: Please describe the DCF model’s approach to estimating the cost of equity. 
12 
A53: The DCF model attempts to estimate the cost of capital for a given company directly, rather 
13 
than based on its risk relative to the market as the CAPM does. The DCF method assumes 
14 
that the market price of a stock is equal to the present value of the dividends that its owners 
15 
expect to receive. The method also assumes that this present value can be calculated by the 
16 
standard formula for the present value of a cash flow—literally a stream of expected “cash 
17 
flows” discounted at a risk-appropriate discount rate. When the cash flows are dividends, 
18 
that discount rate is the cost of equity capital: 
19 
𝑷𝟎ൌ
𝑫𝟏
𝟏ା𝒓൅
𝑫𝟐
ሺ𝟏ା𝒓ሻ𝟐൅
𝑫𝟑
ሺ𝟏ା𝒓ሻ𝟑൅⋯൅
𝑫𝑻
ሺ𝟏ା𝒓ሻ𝑻 
  
(3) 
20 
Where,  
21 
𝑷𝟎 is the current market price of the stock; 
22 
𝑫𝒕 is the dividend cash flow expected at the end of period 𝒕; 
23 
 
86 I round to the nearest 0.25 percent when determining ranges of reasonable results. There are numbers below 10.25 
percent and above 11.25 percent in Figure 15, but if rounding to the nearest 0.25 percent, I have a small number of 
observations above and below the range. I focus on the results obtained from the Hamada approach.  I round to the 
nearest 0.25 percent because the cost of capital cannot, in my opinion, be determined with greater precision. 


--- Page 47 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK  
VILLADSEN DIRECT 
 
 
Page 45 of 57 
𝑻 is the last period in which a dividend cash flow is to be received; and 
1 
𝒓 is the cost of equity capital. 
2 
Importantly, this formula implies that if the current market price and the pattern of expected 
3 
dividends are known, it is possible to “solve for” the discount rate 𝑟 that makes the equation 
4 
true. In this sense, a DCF analysis can be used to estimate the cost of equity capital implied 
5 
by the market price of a stock and market expectations for its future dividends. 
6 
Many DCF applications assume that the growth rate lasts into perpetuity, so the formula can 
7 
be rearranged algebraically to estimate directly the cost of capital. Specifically, the implied 
8 
DCF cost of equity can then be calculated using the well-known “DCF formula” for the cost 
9 
of capital: 
10 
𝒓ൌ
𝑫𝟏
𝑷𝟎൅𝒈ൌ
𝑫𝟎
𝑷𝟎ൈሺ𝟏൅𝒈ሻ൅𝒈 
  
 
 
      (4) 
11 
where 𝑫𝟎 is the current dividend, which investors expect to increase at rate 𝒈 by the end of 
12 
the next period, and over all subsequent periods into perpetuity. 
13 
Equation (4) says that if equation (3) holds, the cost of capital equals the expected dividend 
14 
yield plus the (perpetual) expected future growth rate of dividends. I refer to this as the 
15 
single-stage DCF model; it is also known as the Gordon Growth model, in honor of its 
16 
originator, Professor Myron J Gordon. 
17 
Q54: Are there other versions of the DCF model? 
18 
A54: Yes. There are many alternative versions, notably (i) multi-stage models, (ii) models that use 
19 
cash flow rather than dividends, or versions that combine aspects of (i) and (ii).87 One such 
20 
alternative expands the Gordon Growth model to three stages. In the multistage model, 
21 
 
87 The Surface Transportation Board uses a cash flow based model with three stages.  See, for example, Surface 
Transportation Board Decision, “STB Ex Parte No. 664 (Sub-No. 1),” Decided January 23, 2009.  Confirmed in 
STB EP No. 664 (Sub-No. 4), decided 23, 2020.

earnings and dividends can grow at different rates, but must grow at the same rate in the 
1 
final, constant growth rate period. 88 
2 
In my implementation of the multi-stage DCF, I assume that companies grow their dividend 
3 
for five years at the forecasted company-specific rate of earnings growth, with that growth 
4 
then tapering over the next five years toward the growth rate of the overall economy (i.e., 
5 
the long-term GDP growth rate forecasted to be in effect ten years or more into the future). 
6 
1. DCF Inputs and Results 
7 
Q55: What growth rate information do you use? 
8 
A55: The first step in my DCF analysis (either constant growth or multi-stage formulations) is to 
9 
examine a sample of investment analysts’ forecasted earnings growth rates for companies in 
10 
my proxy group. For the single-stage DCF and for the first stage of the multi-stage DCF, I 
11 
use investment analyst forecasts of company-specific growth rates sourced from Value Line 
12 
and Thomson Reuters IBES. 
13 
For the long-term growth rate for the final, constant-growth stage of the multistage DCF 
14 
estimates, I use the long-term U.S. GDP growth forecast of 4.0 from Blue Chip Economic 
15 
Indicators.89 Thus, the long-run (or terminal) growth rate in the multi-stage model is nominal 
16 
GDP growth. 
17 
Additionally, I relied on the dividend yield of the companies, which I estimate using the most 
18 
recently available dividend information (currently) and the average of the last 15 days of 
19 
stock prices ending October 29, 2021.  As the single largest advantage of the DCF model is 
20 
that it uses current market information, I find it is important to use a relatively short time 
21 
period to determine the dividend yield – yet to avoid the bias caused by any one day.  I 
22

specific versions I implement in this proceeding.

Page 47 of 57 
believe a 15-day average accomplishes that goal. Because some companies engage in share 
1 
buybacks,90 the dividend yield may underestimate the yield on cash distributions to investors.  
2 
Q56: Please explain how input data can affect the DCF models. 
3 
A56: The Gordon Growth/single-stage DCF models require forecast growth rates that reflect 
4 
investor expectations about the pattern of dividend growth for the companies over a 
5 
sufficiently long horizon, but estimates are typically only available for 3-5 years.  
6 
One issue with the data is that it includes solely dividend payments as cash distributions to 
7 
shareholders, while some companies also use share repurchases to distribute cash to 
8 
shareholders.  To the extent that companies in my samples use share repurchases, the DCF 
9 
model using dividend yields will underestimate the cost of equity for these companies.  While 
10 
there are companies in my sample that have engaged in share buybacks in the past, the 
11 
magnitude is currently not large. 
12 
A second issue is that the flight to quality has resulted in higher than usual stock prices for 
13 
electric and natural gas utilities and hence lower than usual dividend yields. As a result, the 
14 
dividend yield may be downward biased. The multi-stage DCF model additionally requires 
15 
a measure of the long-term GDP growth. 
16 
Q57: Please summarize the DCF-based cost of equity estimates for the proxy groups. 
17 
A57: The results of the DCF based estimation for the proxy groups are displayed below in Figure 
18 
16.
